For the zero point of his scale, he used the temperature of a bath of ice in a solution of salt (which was a common way of reaching low temperatures in an 18th-century laboratory). He chose 32°F as the temperature of melting ice (going up) and freezing water (going down). Using this scale of temperature, water boils at 212°F.



# Chapter 13: Turning Up the Heat with Thermodynamics

## Crossing Fahrenheit and Celsius

The Fahrenheit and Celsius scales actually cross at one point, where the temperature in Fahrenheit and Celsius is the same. This point is at $t = -40$. Thus, $-40^\circ C = -40^\circ F$, a rather peculiar fact.

If you do the math, you find 180°F between the points of freezing and boiling in the Fahrenheit system and 100°C in the Celsius system, so the conversion ratio is:

\[
\frac{180}{100} = \frac{18}{10} = \frac{9}{5}
\]

The measurements are also offset by 32 degrees (the 0-degrees point of the Celsius scale corresponds to the 32-degrees point of the Fahrenheit scale). Putting these ideas together allows you to convert from Celsius to Fahrenheit or from Fahrenheit to Celsius using the following equations:

\[
C = \frac{5}{9}(F - 32)
\]
\[
F = \frac{9}{5}C + 32
\]

For example, the blood temperature of a healthy human being is 98.6°F. To convert this to Celsius, plug in the numbers:

\[
C = \frac{5}{9}(F - 32) = \frac{5}{9}(98.6 - 32) = 37.0^\circ C
\]

## When the thermometer says Kelvin

William Thompson created a third temperature system, now commonly used in physics, known as the Kelvin system (he later became Lord Kelvin). The Kelvin system has become so central to physics that the Fahrenheit and Celsius systems are defined in terms of the Kelvin system — a system based on the concept of absolute zero.

## Analyzing absolute zero

Temperature is a measure of molecular movement — how fast and how much the molecules of whatever object you’re measuring are moving. The molecules move more and more slowly as the temperature lowers. At absolute zero, the molecules stop, which means that you can’t cool them any more. No refrigeration system in the world — or in the entire universe — can go any lower.



# Laying Down the Laws of Thermodynamics

The Kelvin system is based on absolute zero as its zero point. Temperature in this scale is measured in kelvins (K). A temperature of 100° in the Celsius system is 100°C, while in the Kelvin scale, it is simply 100 K. The official meters-kilograms-seconds (MKS) unit of temperature is the kelvin.

## Making Kelvin Conversions

Each kelvin is the same size as a Celsius degree. Absolute zero is –273.15°C, which corresponds to 0 K. The formulas for converting between Celsius and Kelvin are:

\[
K = C + 273.15
\]
\[
C = K - 273.15
\]

To convert from kelvins to Fahrenheit, use the formula:

\[
F = \left(\frac{9}{5}\right)(K - 273.15) + 32 = \left(\frac{9}{5}\right)K - 459.67
\]

### Example Calculations

1. **Water Boiling Point in Kelvins**:
\[
K = C + 273.15 = 100 + 273.15 = 373.15 \, K
\]
Water boils at 373.15 K.

2. **Helium Liquefaction Temperature in Celsius**:
\[
C = K - 273.15 = 4.2 - 273.15 = -268.95 \, °C
\]
Helium liquefies at -268.95 °C.

## The Heat Is On: Linear Expansion

Linear expansion refers to the expansion of a solid in one dimension under the influence of heat. For example, screw-top jars can be difficult to open due to thermal expansion.



# Chapter 13: Turning Up the Heat with Thermodynamics

When you apply heat to solids, linear expansion occurs in most cases. The relationship between temperature and length can be described as follows:

If you raise the temperature of an object by a small amount:

$$
T_f = T_0 + \Delta T
$$

where:
- $T_f$ = final temperature
- $T_0$ = original temperature
- $\Delta T$ = change in temperature

This results in an expansion in any linear direction given by:

$$
L_f = L_0 + \Delta L
$$

where:
- $L_f$ = final length of the solid
- $L_0$ = original length
- $\Delta L$ = change in length

Conversely, if the temperature decreases:

$$
T_f = T_0 - \Delta T
$$

you will observe a linear contraction:

$$
L_f = L_0 - \Delta L
$$

This indicates that the change in length, $\Delta L$, is proportional to the change in temperature, $\Delta T$.

However, this proportionality does not hold for all solids. Some materials, like ice, actually contract when heated from 0°C to 4°C due to the rearrangement of their molecular structure from a crystalline form.



```markdown
## Deconstructing Linear Expansion

On a molecular level, linear expansion happens because when you heat objects up, the molecules bounce around faster, which leads to a physical expansion. When you heat up a solid, it expands by a few percent, and that percentage is proportional to the change in temperature, so you can write:

$$
\frac{\Delta L}{L_0} \propto \Delta T
$$

The constant of proportionality depends on what material you’re working with; therefore, in practice, the constant is something you measure, much like the coefficient of friction. And like the coefficient of friction, the constant of proportionality is also a coefficient — the coefficient of linear expansion, which is given by the symbol $\alpha$ (not to be confused with the symbol for angular acceleration). You can write this relationship as an equation this way:

$$
\frac{\Delta L}{L_0} = \alpha \Delta T
$$

You usually write the equation in this form:

$$
\Delta L = \alpha L_0 \Delta T
$$

You usually measure $\alpha$, the coefficient of linear expansion, in $1/°C$ (that is, in °C⁻¹).

## Workin’ on the Railroad: A Linear Expansion Example

Say that you’re called in to check out a new railroad. Given the power generated by the engine, the steepness of the grade, and the mass of the load, you calculate that the engine should be able to make the climb at 1.0 meter per second, no problem.

You look closer at the 10.0-meter-long rails, noticing that they’re only 1 millimeter apart at the ends. You check your almanac, which tells you that you can expect the rails to get 50°C hotter during a normal summer. The coefficient of linear expansion for the steel that the rails are made from is...
```



# Chapter 13: Turning Up the Heat with Thermodynamics

## Linear Expansion

The formula for linear expansion is given by:

\[
\Delta L = \alpha L_0 \Delta T
\]

Where:
- $\Delta L$ = change in length
- $\alpha$ = coefficient of linear expansion (approximately $1.2 \times 10^{-5} \, °C^{-1}$)
- $L_0$ = original length
- $\Delta T$ = change in temperature

For example, if the original length of a rail is $10.0 \, m$ and the temperature change is $50 \, °C$, the change in length can be calculated as follows:

\[
\Delta L = (1.2 \times 10^{-5})(10.0)(50) = 6.0 \times 10^{-3} \, m
\]

This means the rails will expand by $6.0 \times 10^{-3} \, m$, or about $6 \, mm$.

## Volume Expansion

Volume expansion occurs in three dimensions and can be expressed as:

\[
\frac{\Delta V}{V_0} = \beta \Delta T
\]

Where:
- $\Delta V$ = change in volume
- $V_0$ = original volume
- $\beta$ = coefficient of volume expansion (measured in $°C^{-1}$)
- $\Delta T$ = change in temperature

Rearranging this equation gives:

\[
\Delta V = \beta V_0 \Delta T
\]



```markdown
You’ve created the analog (or equivalent) of the equation

$$
\Delta L = \alpha L_0 \Delta T
$$

for linear expansion.

If the distances involved are small and the temperature changes are small, you usually find that

$$
\beta = 3\alpha.
$$

This makes sense, because you go from one dimension to three. For example, $\alpha$ for steel is $1.2 \times 10^{-5} \, \text{°C}^{-1}$, and $\beta = 3.6 \times 10^{-5} \, \text{°C}^{-1}$.

Say, for example, you’re at the gasoline refinery when you notice that workers are filling all the 5,000-gallon tanker trucks to the very brim before leaving the refinery on a hot summer day.

For gasoline, $\beta = 9.5 \times 10^{-4} \, \text{°C}^{-1}$, and you figure that it’s 10°C warmer in the sunshine than in the building, so

$$
\Delta V = \beta V_0 \Delta T = (9.5 \times 10^{-4})(5,000)(10) = 47.5 \, \text{gallons}.
$$

Not good news for the refinery — those 5,000-gallon tankers of gasoline that are filled to the brim have to carry 5,047.5 gallons of gasoline after they go out in the sunshine.

### Going with the Flow (of Heat)

What, really, is heat? When you touch a hot object, heat flows from the object to you, and your nerves record that fact. When you touch a cold object, heat flows from you to that object, and again, your nerves keep track of what’s happening. Your nerves record why objects feel hot or cold — because heat flows from them to you or from you to them.

So, what is heat in physics terms? Heat is energy that flows from objects of higher temperatures to objects of lower temperatures. The unit of this energy in the meters-kilograms-seconds (MKS) system is the Joule (J).
```



# Chapter 13: Turning Up the Heat with Thermodynamics

Where does this energy come from? Heat is a measure of the energy stored in the internal molecular motion of an object. Heat stays in an object until it can leak out to the environment.

Different materials can hold different amounts of heat. For example, a potato can hold its heat longer than a lighter material like cotton candy. The measure of how much heat an object can hold is called its specific heat capacity.

Physicists measure everything, so it’s common to use a thermometer when making a cup of coffee. If you measure 1.0 kg of coffee in the pot, you find that it takes 4,186 J of heat energy to raise the temperature of the coffee by 1°C, while it takes only 840 J to raise 1.0 kg of glass by 1°C. The energy goes into the object being heated, which stores it as internal energy until it leaks out again. If it takes 4,186 J to raise 1.0 kg of coffee by 1°C, it takes double that, 8,372 J, to raise 2.0 kg of coffee by 1°C.

You can relate the amount of heat, $Q$, it takes to raise the temperature of an object to the change in temperature and the amount of mass involved:

$$
Q = cm\Delta T
$$

where:
- $Q$ is the amount of heat energy involved (measured in Joules in the MKS system),
- $m$ is the amount of mass,
- $\Delta T$ is the change in temperature, and
- $c$ is a constant called the specific heat capacity, measured in J/(kg-°C) in the MKS system.

One calorie is defined as the amount of heat needed to heat 1.0 gram of water by 1.0°C, so $1 \text{ calorie} = 4,186 \text{ J}$. Nutritionists use the food-energy term Calorie (capital C) to stand for 1,000 calories — $1.0 \text{ kcal} = 4,186 \text{ J}$. Physicists also use the British Thermal Unit (Btu), where $1 \text{ Btu}$ is the amount of heat needed to raise 1 pound of water by 1.0°F. The conversion is $1 \text{ Btu} = 1,055 \text{ J}$.

Now, if the 45 g of coffee in your cup is cold at 45°C, but you prefer it at 65°C, you can calculate how much more coffee to pour from the pot at 95°C.

The heat lost by the new mass of coffee, $m_1$, is represented as:

$$
\Delta Q_1 = cm_1 (T_f - T_{1o})
$$

The heat gained by the existing coffee, mass $m_2$, is represented as:

$$
\Delta Q_2 = cm_2 (T_f - T_{2o})
$$



```markdown
## Laying Down the Laws of Thermodynamics

Assuming you have a super-insulating coffee mug, the heat lost by the new coffee is the heat that the existing coffee will gain, so

$$
\Delta Q_1 = -\Delta Q_2
$$

which means that

$$
-c m_1 (T_f - T_{1o}) = c m_2 (T_f - T_{2o})
$$

Dividing both sides by the specific heat capacity and plugging in the numbers gives you

$$
-m_1 (65 - 95) = -m_1 (-30) = m_1(30)
$$
$$
m_2 (T_f - T_{2o}) = (0.045)(65 - 45) = 0.9
$$
$$
m_1(30) = 0.9
$$

Dividing both sides by 30 gives you

$$
\frac{m_1(30)}{30} = \frac{0.9}{30} = m_1 = 0.03 \text{ kg} = 30 \text{ g}
$$

Satisfied, you put away your calculator and say, “Give me exactly 30.0 grams of that coffee.”

### Changing Phases: When Temperatures Don’t Change

The equation of the moment is

$$
Q = cm\Delta T
$$

— used to find the amount of heat energy with respect to the change in temperature — but you also come across cases where adding or removing heat doesn’t change the temperature. For example, imagine you’re calmly drinking your lemonade at an outdoor garden party. You grab some ice to cool your lemonade, and the mixture in your glass is now half ice, half lemonade (which you can assume has the same specific heat as water; see the previous section), with a temperature of exactly 0°C.

As you hold the glass and watch the action, the ice begins to melt — but the contents of the glass don’t change temperature. Why? The heat going into the glass is melting the ice, not warming the mixture up. So, does this make the equation for heat energy useless? Not at all. It just means that the equation doesn’t apply for a phase change.

Phase changes occur when materials change state, from liquid to solid (as when water freezes), solid to liquid (as when rocks melt into lava), liquid to gas (as when you boil tea), and so on. When the material in question changes to a new state — liquid, solid, or gas (you can also factor in a fourth state: plasma, a superheated gas-like state) — some heat goes into or comes out of the process.
```



```markdown
You can even have solids that turn directly into gas, as when you have a block of dry ice, frozen carbon dioxide. As the dry ice gets warmer, it turns into carbon dioxide gas. This process is called sublimation.

### Breaking the ice with phase changes

Imagine that someone has taken a bag of ice and thoughtlessly put it on the stove. Before it hit the stove, the ice was at a temperature below freezing (–5°C), but being on the stove is about to change that.

#### Water boils

#### Ice melts

As long as no phase change takes place, the equation

$$ Q = cm\Delta T $$

holds good, where the specific heat capacity of ice is around $2.0 \times 10^{-3} \, \text{J/(kg-°C)}$. This means that the temperature of the ice will increase linearly as you add more heat to it.

However, when the ice reaches 0°C, you start to get nervous. The ice is getting too warm to hold its solid state, and it begins to melt, undergoing a phase change. On a molecular level, it takes energy to make an object change state. For example, when you melt ice, it takes energy to break up the crystalline ice structure. The energy needed to melt the ice is supplied as heat. This is why the graph levels off in the middle — the ice is melting. It takes heat to make the ice change phase to water, so even though the stove adds heat, the temperature of the ice doesn’t change as it melts.

As you watch the bag of ice on the stove, you note that all the ice eventually melts into water. Because the stove is still adding heat, the water begins to heat up.
```



# Laying Down the Laws of Thermodynamics

## Understanding Latent Heat

Latent heat is the heat per kilogram that you have to add or remove to make an object change its state; in other words, latent heat is the heat needed to make a phase change happen. Its units are J/kg in the meters-kilograms-seconds (MKS) system.

Physics recognizes three types of latent heat, corresponding to the changes of phase between solid, liquid, and gas:

- **The latent heat of fusion, $L_f$**: The heat per kilogram needed to make the change between the solid to liquid phases, as when water turns to ice.
- **The latent heat of vaporization, $L_v$**: The heat per kilogram needed to make the change between the liquid to gas phases, as when water boils.
- **The latent heat of sublimation, $L_s$**: The heat per kilogram needed to make the change between the solid to gas phases, as when dry ice evaporates.

For example, the latent heat of fusion of water, $L_f$, is $3.35 \times 10^5 \, \text{J/kg}$, and the latent heat of vaporization of water, $L_v$, is $2.26 \times 10^6 \, \text{J/kg}$. In other words, it takes $3.35 \times 10^5$ Joules to melt 1 kilogram of ice at $0^\circ C$ (just to melt it, not to change its temperature). And it takes $2.26 \times 10^6$ Joules to boil 1 kilogram of water into steam. These are the energies involved in making water change its phase.



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

## Heat Transfer Mechanisms

Heat can be transferred through three primary mechanisms: conduction, convection, and radiation.

### Convection

Convection is a primary means of heat transfer that occurs in fluids (liquids and gases). When a fluid is heated, it becomes less dense and rises, while the cooler, denser fluid sinks. This creates a circulation pattern that transfers heat throughout the fluid.

For example, when heating a pot of water, the water near the heating element heats up, expands, and becomes less dense. This less-dense water rises to the surface, while cooler water descends to take its place, creating a convection current.

### Conduction

Conduction is the transfer of heat through a material without the movement of the material itself. It occurs when two objects at different temperatures are in direct contact. Heat flows from the hotter object to the cooler one until thermal equilibrium is reached.

The rate of heat transfer by conduction can be described by Fourier's law:

$$
q = -k \frac{dT}{dx}
$$

where:
- \( q \) is the heat transfer per unit time,
- \( k \) is the thermal conductivity of the material,
- \( \frac{dT}{dx} \) is the temperature gradient.

### Radiation

Radiation is the transfer of heat in the form of electromagnetic waves. Unlike conduction and convection, radiation does not require a medium to transfer heat. All objects emit radiation based on their temperature, described by Stefan-Boltzmann law:

$$
E = \sigma T^4
$$

where:
- \( E \) is the radiant energy emitted per unit area,
- \( \sigma \) is the Stefan-Boltzmann constant,
- \( T \) is the absolute temperature in Kelvin.

## Gases and Heat

When discussing gases, it is essential to understand the concept of moles and Avogadro's number, which is approximately \( 6.022 \times 10^{23} \) particles per mole. This number allows us to relate the amount of substance to the number of molecules present.

### Ideal Gas Law

The behavior of ideal gases can be described by the ideal gas law:

$$
PV = nRT
$$

where:
- \( P \) is the pressure,
- \( V \) is the volume,
- \( n \) is the number of moles,
- \( R \) is the ideal gas constant,
- \( T \) is the temperature in Kelvin.

This equation helps predict how gases will behave under different conditions of temperature and pressure.

## Conclusion

Understanding heat transfer mechanisms and the behavior of gases is crucial in various scientific and practical applications. By mastering these concepts, one can better comprehend everyday phenomena and engage in more complex scientific discussions.



```markdown
## Convection

Convection is a heat transfer process that relies on the movement of a medium, such as water or air. For example, when boiling a pot of water, you can observe convection as the noodles circulate. This process does not require water specifically; air and other substances can also facilitate convection.

Birds often ride thermals, which are columns of rising hot air created by convective motion near the Earth's surface. Many ovens utilize convection; for instance, a convection oven circulates hot air to cook food, unlike a microwave oven, which heats food by polarizing molecules through microwaves.

The principle of "heat rises" is central to convection. Hot air expands, becoming less dense than cooler air, causing it to rise. In a multi-story house, heated air tends to accumulate on the upper floors during winter. Radiators also rely on convection to circulate warm air, and ceiling fans can enhance this movement.

## Conduction

Conduction is the transfer of heat through a material without the need for currents, unlike convection. For instance, if a metal pot has been boiling for 15 minutes, its handle will also become hot due to conduction. Grabbing the handle without protection, such as an oven mitt, can result in burns.
```



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

The metal handle is hot, but why? Not because of convection, because no heat-driven currents of mass are at work here. The handle is hot because of conduction.

On the molecular level, the molecules near the heat source are heated and begin moving faster. They bounce off the molecules near them and cause them to move faster. That increased bouncing is what heats a substance up.

Some materials, like most metals, conduct heat better than others, such as porcelain, wood, or glass. The way substances conduct heat depends a great degree on their molecular structures, so different substances react differently.

## Examining the properties that affect conduction to find the conduction equation

You have to take different properties of objects into account when you want to examine the conduction that takes place. If you have a bar of steel, for example, you have to take into account the bar’s area and length, along with the temperature at different parts of the bar.

The amount of heat transferred, $Q$, is proportional to the difference in temperature, $\Delta T$:

$$
Q \propto \Delta T
$$



```markdown
On the other hand, as you may expect, a bar twice as wide conducts twice the amount of heat. In general, the amount of heat conducted, $Q$, is proportional to the cross-sectional area, $A$, like this:

$$
Q \propto A
$$

And the longer the bar, the less the amount of heat that will make it all the way through; in fact, the conducted heat turns out to be inversely proportional to the length of the bar, $L$:

$$
Q \propto \frac{1}{L}
$$

Finally, the amount of heat transferred, $Q$, depends on the amount of time that passes, $t$ — twice the time, twice the heat. Here’s how you express this mathematically:

$$
Q \propto t
$$

When you put it all together and nominate $k$ as the constant that’s yet to be determined, the heat transfer by conduction through a material is given by this equation:

$$
Q = \frac{kA\Delta T t}{L}
$$

This represents the amount of heat transferred by conduction in a given amount of time, $t$, down a length $L$, where the cross-sectional area is $A$. Here, $k$ is the material’s thermal conductivity, measured in Joules/(s-m-°C).

Different materials (glass, steel, copper, bubble gum) conduct heat at different rates, so the thermal conductivity constant depends on the material in question.
```



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

## Thermal Conductivities for Various Materials

| Material   | Thermal Conductivity (J/(s-m-°C)) |
|------------|------------------------------------|
| Glass      | 0.80                               |
| Steel      | 14.0                               |
| Copper     | 390                                |
| Styrofoam  | 0.01                               |
| Brass      | 110                                |
| Silver     | 420                                |

## Applying the Heat-Transferred-by-Conduction Equation

The thermal conductivity of the steel part of a pot handle is 14.0 Joules/(s-m-°C). If that handle is 15 cm long, with a cross-sectional area of 2.0 cm² (2.0 × 10⁻⁴ m²), and the fire at one end is 600°C, the heat transfer can be calculated using the equation for heat transfer by conduction:

\[
Q = \frac{kA\Delta T t}{L}
\]

Assuming that the cool end of the handle starts at about room temperature (25°C), we can substitute the values into the equation:

\[
Q = \frac{(14)(2.0 \times 10^{-4})(600 - 25)t}{0.15} = (10.7J)t
\]

## Becoming a Cooler Engineer

Based on the thermal conducting or insulating abilities of different materials, you can calculate how to keep objects cold or hot. For example, if you were to design a frozen food storage locker, using copper (thermal conductivity of 390 J/(s-m-°C)) would not be effective, as it allows heat to move in or out easily.

In contrast, Styrofoam, with a thermal conductivity of only 0.01 J/(s-m-°C), would be a much better choice. If ice lasts a time \( t \) in the copper locker, it will last approximately:

\[
\frac{390}{0.01} = 39,000 \text{ times longer}
\]

if you build the cooler with Styrofoam instead.



# Laying Down the Laws of Thermodynamics

You find that 10.7 Joules (the unit of measure in question) of heat are being transferred to the end of the handle each second. And as the seconds go by, the Joules of heat will add up, making the handle hotter and hotter.

## Emitting and Absorbing Light: Radiation

You get out of the shower soaking wet in the dead of winter, and you’re toasty warm. Why? Because of a little physics, of course. In particular, the heat lamp you have in your bathroom. The heat lamp beams out heat to you and keeps you warm through radiation.

Radiation is light that can transfer heat. Heat energy transferred through radiation is as familiar as the light of day; in fact, it is the light of day. The sun is a huge thermal reactor about 93 million miles away in space, and neither conduction nor convection would produce any of the energy that arrives to Earth through the vacuum of space. The sun’s energy gets to the Earth through radiation, which you can confirm on a sunny day just by standing outside and letting the sun’s rays warm your face. The only way you can actually see radiation, however, is through the sunburn you later have to manage.



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

## Radiation

Every object around you is continually radiating, unless it’s at absolute zero temperature. A scoop of ice cream, for example, radiates. Electromagnetic radiation comes from accelerating and decelerating electric charges, which occurs as objects warm up — their atoms move around faster and bounce off other atoms.

Humans radiate heat in all directions, and everything in the environment radiates heat back. When you have the same temperature as your surroundings, you radiate as fast and as much to your environment as it does to you. If your environment didn’t radiate heat back, you would freeze, which is why space is considered so “cold.” In space, heat isn’t lost through conduction or convection; it’s lost because the environment doesn’t radiate back.

When an object heats up to about 1,000 K, it starts to glow red. As the object gets hotter, its radiation moves up in the spectrum through orange, yellow, and so on, reaching white hot at around 1,700 K.

Radiant heaters with coils that glow red rely on radiation to transfer heat. Convection occurs as air gets heated, rises, and spreads around the room. Conduction can occur if you touch a hot spot on the heater. However, heat transfer primarily takes place through radiation. Many houses utilize radiant heating, where heating wires are buried in walls, ceilings, or floors, providing warmth without visible elements.

## Heat Absorption and Reflection

Humans intuitively understand heat radiation and absorption. For example, on a hot day, wearing a black t-shirt is avoided because it absorbs more light from the environment and reflects less than a white t-shirt. The white t-shirt keeps you cooler by reflecting more radiant heat back to the environment.



```markdown
## Reflecting on cold metal

Why do metals feel cold to the touch? When you pick up a metal object (even one you don’t find in the refrigerator, such as a can of soda), it feels cold to the touch. The answer lies in radiant heat. Metals are shiny, so they can reflect back the radiant heat hitting them better than other objects in a room, even white ones. So, if a spoon reflects back 90 percent of the radiant heat hitting it, and a book reflects back just 10 percent, which feels cooler to the touch? Metal, indeed.

### Radiation and blackbodies

Some objects absorb more of the light that hits them than others. The objects that absorb all the radiant heat that strikes them have a name — they’re called blackbodies. A blackbody absorbs 100 percent of the radiant heat striking it, and if it’s in equilibrium with its surroundings, it emits all the radiant heat as well.

Most objects fall between mirrors, which reflect all light, and blackbodies, which absorb all light. The middle-of-the-road objects absorb some of the light striking them and emit it back into their surroundings. Shiny objects are shiny because they reflect most of the light, which means they don’t have to emit as much heat radiantly into the room as other objects. Dark objects appear dark because they don’t reflect much light, which means they have to emit more as radiant heat (usually lower down in the spectrum, where light is infrared and can’t be seen).

You can find plenty of physics when it comes to blackbodies, starting with the following question: How much heat does a blackbody emit when it’s at a certain temperature? The amount of heat radiated is proportional to the time you allow — twice as long, twice as much heat radiated, for example. So, you can write the equation, where $t$ is time, as follows:

$$
Q \propto t
$$

And as you may expect, the amount of heat radiated is proportional to the total area doing the radiating. So, you can also write the equation as follows, where $A$ is the area doing the radiating:

$$
Q \propto At
$$
```



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

Temperature, $T$, has to be in the equation somewhere — the hotter an object, the more heat radiated. Experimentally, it turns out that the amount of heat radiated is proportional to $T$ to the fourth power, $T^4$. So, now you have

$$
Q \propto A t T^4
$$

To make the equation final, you need to add a constant, which is measured experimentally. To find the heat emitted by a blackbody, you use the Stefan-Boltzmann constant, $\sigma$, which goes in like this:

$$
Q = \sigma A t T^4
$$

The value of $\sigma$ is $5.67 \times 10^{-8} \, \text{J} \, \text{(s-m}^2\text{-K}^4)$. Note, however, that this constant works only for blackbodies that are perfect emitters. Most objects aren’t perfect emitters, so you have to add another constant most of the time — one that depends on the substance you’re working with. The constant is called emissivity, $e$. So, this equation — the Stefan-Boltzmann law of radiation — becomes

$$
Q = e \sigma A t T^4
$$

where $e$ is an object’s emissivity, $\sigma$ is the Stefan-Boltzmann constant ($5.67 \times 10^{-8} \, \text{J} \, \text{(s-m}^2\text{-K}^4)$), $A$ is the radiating area, $t$ is time, and $T$ is the temperature in kelvins.

For example, a person’s emissivity is about 0.8. At a body temperature of $37^\circ C$, how much heat does a person radiate each second? First, you have to factor in how much area does the radiating. If you consider a person mathematically as a 1.6-meter-high cylinder of radius 0.1m, the total surface area would be

$$
A = \text{(area of a cylinder)} = 2\pi rh + 2\pi r^2 = 2\pi (0.1)(1.6) + 2\pi(0.1)^2 = 1.07 \, \text{m}^2
$$

where $r$ is the radius and $h$ is height. To find the total heat radiated by a person, plug the numbers into the Stefan-Boltzmann law of radiation equation:

$$
Q = e \sigma A t T^4 = (0.8)(5.67 \times 10^{-8})(1.07)(37 + 273.15)^4 t = 449t
$$

You get a value of 449 Joules per second, or 449 watts. That may seem high, because skin temperature isn’t the same as internal body temperature, but it’s in the ballpark.



# Crunching Avogadro’s Number

Much of the info in this chapter deals with the effects of heat on solids and liquids (as in pools of liquid metal, for example), but you can examine plenty of physics when you heat up gases, too. It all starts by knowing how many molecules of a substance you’re dealing with, which you can figure out by using a little physics.

For example, consider the scenario of a person who finds a large diamond and brings it to you for examination.

“How many atoms are in my diamond?”

“That depends,” you say, “on how many moles you have.”

A mole is the number of atoms in 12.0 grams of carbon isotope 12. Carbon isotope 12 — also called carbon-12, or just carbon 12 — is the most common version of carbon, although some carbon atoms have a few more neutrons in them — carbon 13, actually — so the average works out to about 12.011. You have to know how many moles of matter you have before you can know how many atoms you have, and an atom-by-atom knowledge is essential when you start working with heat and gases.

The number of atoms has been measured as $6.022 \times 10^{23}$, which is called Avogadro’s Number, $N_A$. So, now you know how many atoms are in 12.0 grams of carbon 12. Do you find the same number of atoms in, say, 12.0 grams of sulphur? Nope. Each sulphur atom has a different mass from each carbon atom, so even if you have the same number of grams, you have a different number of atoms.

How much more mass does sulphur have than carbon 12? If you check a periodic table of elements hanging on the wall in a physics lab, you’ll find that the atomic mass of sulphur (usually the number appearing right under the element’s symbol, like S for sulphur) is 32.06. But 32.06 what? It’s 32.06 atomic units, $u$, where each atomic unit is $\frac{1}{12}$ of the mass of a carbon 12 atom. If a mole of carbon 12 has a mass of 12.0 grams, therefore, and the mass of your average sulphur atom is bigger than the mass of a carbon 12 atom by the following ratio:

$$
\text{Sulphur mass} = 32.06
$$
$$
\text{Carbon 12 mass} = 12 \, u
$$

A mole of sulphur atoms must have this mass:

$$
\left(\frac{32.06}{12 \, u}\right)(12.0 \, g) = 32.06 \, g
$$



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

How convenient! Knowing that a mole of an element has the same mass in grams as its atomic mass in atomic units proves helpful in your calculations. You can read the atomic mass of any element in atomic units off any periodic table. You can find that a mole of silicon has a mass of 28.09 grams, a mole of sodium has a mass of 22.99 grams, and so on. And each of those moles contains $6.022 \times 10^{23}$ atoms.

Now you can determine the number of atoms in a diamond, which is solid carbon (atomic mass = 12.01 u). A mole is 12.01 g of diamond, so when you find out how many moles you have, you multiply that times $6.022 \times 10^{23}$ atoms.

Not every object is made up of a single kind of atom. Most materials are composites, such as water, which is made up of two hydrogen atoms for every one oxygen atom (H₂O). In cases like these, instead of the atomic mass, you look for the molecular mass (when atoms combine, you have molecules), which is also measured in atomic mass units. For example, the molecular mass of water is 18.0153 u, so 1 mole of water molecules has a mass of 18.0153 g.

## Forging the Ideal Gas Law

When you start working on an atom-by-atom and molecule-by-molecule level, you begin working with gases from a physics point of view. For example, how do moles factor into predicting what’s going to happen when you heat gases? It turns out that you can relate the temperature, pressure, volume, and number of moles together for a gas. The relation doesn’t always hold completely true, but it always does for ideal gases. Ideal gases are very light, like helium, and they’re the frictionless ramps of thermodynamics.

It’s an experimental fact that if you keep the volume constant and heat a gas, the pressure will go up linearly. In other words, at a constant volume, where $T$ is the temperature measured in kelvins and $P$ is the pressure,

$$ P \propto T $$

You can let the volume vary as well, and if you do, you’ll find that the pressure is inversely proportional to the volume — if the volume of a gas doubles, its pressure will go down by a factor of two:

$$ P \propto \frac{T}{V} $$



```markdown
On the other hand, when the volume and temperature of an ideal gas are constant, the pressure is proportional to the number of moles of gas you have — twice the amount of gas, twice the pressure. If the number of moles is $n$,

$$
P \propto \frac{nT}{V}
$$

Adding a constant, $R$ — the universal gas constant, which has a value of 8.31 Joules(mole-K) — gives you the ideal gas law, which relates pressure, volume, number of moles, and temperature:

$$
PV = nRT
$$

Ideal gases are those gases for which the ideal gas law holds. By using the ideal gas law, you can predict the pressure of an ideal gas, given how much you have of it, its temperature, and the volume you’ve enclosed it in.

You can also express the ideal gas law a little differently by using Avogadro’s Number, $N_A$, and the total number of molecules, $N$:

$$
PV = nRT = \left(\frac{N}{N_A}\right) RT
$$

The constant $\frac{R}{N_A}$ is also called Boltzmann’s constant, $k$, and has a value of $1.38 \times 10^{-23} \text{ J/K}$. Using this constant, the ideal gas law becomes

$$
PV = NkT
$$
```



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

## Gas pressure: An ideal gas law example

Say that you’re measuring a volume of 1 cubic meter filled with 600 moles of helium (which is very close to an ideal gas) at room temperature, 27°C. What would be the pressure of the gas? Using the following form of the ideal gas law:

\[
PV = nRT
\]

you can put \( P \) on one side by dividing by \( V \). Now plug in the numbers:

\[
P = \frac{nRT}{V} = \frac{(600.0)(8.31)(273.15 + 27)}{1.0} = 1.50 \times 10^{6} \, \text{N/m}^2
\]

The pressure on all the walls of the container is \( 1.50 \times 10^{6} \, \text{N/m}^2 \). Notice the units of pressure here — N/m². The unit is used so commonly that it has its own name in the MKS system: Pascals, or Pa. One Pa = \( 1.45 \times 10^{-4} \) pounds per inch². Atmospheric pressure is \( 1.013 \times 10^{5} \, \text{Pa} \), which is \( 14.70 \, \text{lb/in}^2 \). The pressure of 1 atmosphere is also given in torr on occasion, and \( 1.0 \) atmosphere = \( 760 \, \text{torr} \). In this example, you have a pressure of \( 1.50 \times 10^{6} \, \text{Pa} \), which is about 15 atmospheres.

You may come across a special set of conditions sometimes used when talking about gases — Standard Temperature and Pressure, also called STP. The STP pressure is \( 1 \, \text{atmosphere} \), \( 1.013 \times 10^{5} \, \text{Pa} \), and the STP temperature is \( 0 \, \text{°C} \). You can use the ideal gas law to calculate that at STP, \( 1.0 \) mole of an ideal gas occupies \( 22.4 \) liters of volume (1.0 liter is \( 1 \times 10^{-3} \, \text{m}^3 \)).

## Boyle’s Law and Charles’ Law: Alternative expressions of the ideal gas law

You can often express the ideal gas law in different ways. For example, you can express the relationship between the pressure and volume of an ideal gas before and after one of those quantities changes at a constant temperature like this:

\[
P_f V_f = P_0 V_0
\]

This equation, called Boyle’s Law, says that, all factors being the same, the product \( PV \) will be conserved. Similarly, at constant pressure, you can say that

\[
\frac{V_f}{T_f} = \frac{V_0}{T_0}
\]

This equation, called Charles’ Law, says that the ratio of \( V/T \) will be conserved for an ideal gas, all factors being the same.



```markdown
## Measuring Water and Air Pressure

You can determine the pressure under fluids like water with a simple equation:

$$
P = \rho gh
$$

Here, $\rho$ is the density of the fluid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity (9.8 m/s²), and $h$ is the depth in meters. For water, $\rho$ is about 1,000 kg/m³, so you get:

$$
P = (1,000.0)(9.8)h = 9,800h
$$

For every meter you go under water, you gain about 9,800 Pa of pressure, or about 1/10 of an atmosphere. This equation works as long as the density of the fluid doesn’t vary, which means that it’s hard to predict what the pressure due to air will be, because the density of air varies all the way out to space.

## Tracking Ideal Gas Molecules

You can calculate the average kinetic energy of each molecule of an ideal gas with the equation:

$$
KE_{avg} = \frac{3}{2} k T
$$

where $k$ is Boltzmann’s constant, $1.38 \times 10^{-23} \text{ J/K}$, and $T$ is the temperature. You can determine the mass of each molecule if you know what gas you’re dealing with, allowing you to figure out the molecules’ speeds at various temperatures.
```



# Chapter 14: Here, Take My Coat: Heat Transfer in Solids and Gases

For example, you can’t see the air molecules whizzing around you, but you can predict their average speeds. You get out your calculator and thermometer. You measure the air temperature at about 28°C, or 301 kelvin. You know that for the molecules in the air, you can measure their average kinetic energy with

$$
KE_{avg} = \frac{3}{2}kT
$$

Now plug in the numbers:

$$
KE_{avg} = \frac{3}{2}kT = \frac{3}{2}(1.38 \times 10^{-23})(301) = 6.23 \times 10^{-21} \, J
$$

The average molecule has a kinetic energy of $6.23 \times 10^{-21}$ Joules. On the other hand, the molecules are pretty small — what speed does $6.23 \times 10^{-21}$ Joules correspond to? Well, you know that

$$
KE = \frac{1}{2}mv^2
$$

where $m$ is the mass and $v$ is the velocity, so

$$
v = \sqrt{\frac{2KE}{m}}
$$

Air is mostly nitrogen, and each nitrogen atom has a mass of about $4.65 \times 10^{-26} \, kg$. You can plug in the numbers to get

$$
v = \sqrt{\frac{2KE}{m}} = \sqrt{\frac{2(6.23 \times 10^{-21})}{4.65 \times 10^{-26}}} \approx 517 \, m/s \approx 1,150 \, miles/hour
$$

Yow! What an image; huge numbers of the little guys crashing into you at 1,150 miles per hour every second! Good thing for you the molecules are so small. Imagine if each air molecule weighed a couple of pounds. Big problems.

## Calculating kinetic energy in an ideal gas

It’s true that atoms have very little mass, but there are many, many of them in gases, and because they all have kinetic energy, the total kinetic energy can pile up pretty fast. How much total kinetic energy can you find in a certain amount of gas? Each molecule has this average kinetic energy:

$$
KE_{avg} = \frac{3}{2}kT
$$



```markdown
To figure the total kinetic energy, you multiply by the number of molecules you have, which is $nN_A$, where $n$ is the number of moles:

$$ KE_{\text{total}} = \frac{3}{2} n N_A k T $$

$N_A k$ equals $R$, the universal gas constant, so this equation becomes

$$ KE_{\text{total}} = \frac{3}{2} n R T $$

If you have 600 moles of helium at 27°C, you have this much internal energy wrapped up in thermal movement:

$$ KE_{\text{total}} = \frac{3}{2} n R T = \frac{3}{2} (600.0)(8.31)(273.15 + 27) = 2.24 \times 10^6 \, \text{J} $$

This converts to a little less than 1 Calorie (the kind of energy unit you find on food wrappers).
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

## Gaining Thermal Equilibrium: The Zeroth Law of Thermodynamics

The laws of thermodynamics start with the zeroth law. The zeroth law of thermodynamics states that two objects are in thermal equilibrium if heat can pass between them, but no heat is doing so.

For example, if you and the swimming pool you’re in are at the same temperature, no heat is flowing from you to it or from it to you (although the possibility is there). You’re in thermal equilibrium.

## The First Law of Thermodynamics: Conservation of Energy

The first law of thermodynamics is a statement of the conservation of energy. It states that energy cannot be created or destroyed, only transformed from one form to another. Mathematically, this can be expressed as:

$$ \Delta U = Q - W $$

where:
- $\Delta U$ is the change in internal energy of the system,
- $Q$ is the heat added to the system, and
- $W$ is the work done by the system.

This law implies that the total energy of an isolated system remains constant.

## The Second Law of Thermodynamics: Heat Flow

The second law of thermodynamics introduces the concept of entropy, stating that in any energy transfer, the total entropy of a closed system can never decrease over time. This law can be expressed in terms of heat flow:

Heat naturally flows from a hotter object to a cooler one, and this flow continues until thermal equilibrium is reached.

## The Third Law of Thermodynamics: Absolute Zero

The third law of thermodynamics states that as the temperature of a system approaches absolute zero, the entropy of a perfect crystal approaches zero. Absolute zero is defined as 0 Kelvin, or -273.15 degrees Celsius.

This law implies that it is impossible to reach absolute zero in a finite number of steps.



```markdown
## Conserving Heat and Energy: The First Law of Thermodynamics

The first law of thermodynamics is a statement of energy conservation. It states that energy is conserved. The internal energy in a system, $U_0$, changes to a final value $U_f$ when heat, $Q$, is absorbed or released by the system and the system does work, $W$, on its surroundings (or the surroundings do work on the system), such that

$$
U_f - U_0 = \Delta U = Q - W
$$

These three quantities — heat, work, and internal energy — make up all the energy you need to consider, so their sum is conserved. When you add heat transfer, $Q$, to a system, and that system doesn’t work, the amount of internal energy changes accordingly.
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

The first law of thermodynamics states that the change in internal energy ($U$) of a system is equal to the heat added to the system ($Q$) minus the work done by the system ($W$):

\[
\Delta U = Q - W
\]

- $Q$ is positive when heat is absorbed by the system and negative when heat is released.
- $W$ is positive when the system does work on its surroundings and negative when work is done on the system.

### Example Calculation

If a motor does 2,000 Joules of work on its surroundings while releasing 3,000 Joules of heat, the change in internal energy can be calculated as follows:

1. Work done by the system: $W = 2,000 \, \text{J}$
2. Heat released by the system: $Q = -3,000 \, \text{J}$

The change in internal energy ($\Delta U$) is:

\[
\Delta U = -2,000 - 3,000 = -5,000 \, \text{J}
\]

This indicates that the internal energy of the system decreases by 5,000 J.

### Alternative Scenario

If the system absorbs 3,000 J of heat from the surroundings while doing 2,000 J of work on those surroundings, the calculation would be:

1. Heat absorbed by the system: $Q = 3,000 \, \text{J}$
2. Work done by the system: $W = 2,000 \, \text{J}$

The change in internal energy ($\Delta U$) is:

\[
\Delta U = -2,000 + 3,000 = 1,000 \, \text{J}
\]

In this case, the net change to the system’s internal energy is +1,000 J.



```markdown
that its surroundings perform 4,000 J of work on the system. You can tell that both of these energies will flow into the system, so its internal energy goes up by 3,000 J + 4,000 J = 7,000 J. If you want to go by the numbers, use this equation:

$$
\Delta U = Q - W
$$

and then note that because the surroundings are doing work on the system, $W$ is considered negative. So, you get

$$
\Delta U = Q - W = 3,000 - (-4,000) = 7,000 \, \text{J}
$$

Examining isobaric, isochoric, isothermal, and adiabatic processes, oh my!

You come across a number of quantities floating around in this chapter — volume, pressure, temperature, and so on — and the way in which these quantities vary as you do work affects the results. For example, if a gas is doing work while you keep its volume constant, the process is different than when you keep its pressure constant.

There are four standard conditions under which work is performed in thermodynamics: Constant pressure, constant volume, constant temperature, and constant heat.

Note that when anything changes in the processes in the following sections, the change is assumed to be quasi-static, which means the change comes slowly enough that the pressure and temperature are the same throughout the system’s volume.

### At constant pressure: Isobaric

When you have a process where the pressure stays constant, it’s called isobaric (“baric” means pressure). In Figure 15-1, you see a cylinder with a piston being lifted by a quantity of gas as the gas gets hotter. The volume of the gas is changing, but the weighted piston keeps the pressure constant.

What work is the system doing as the gas expands? Work equals $F$ times $s$, where $F$ represents force and $s$ represents distance, and Force equals $P$ times $A$, where $P$ is the pressure and $A$ is the area. This means that

$$
W = F \Delta s = PA \Delta s
$$
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

In an isobaric process, the pressure remains constant while the volume changes. The work done by the system can be expressed as:

$$
W = P \Delta V
$$

where \( W \) is the work done, \( P \) is the constant pressure, and \( \Delta V \) is the change in volume.

Graphically, the isobaric process can be represented in a pressure-volume graph, where the area beneath the graph corresponds to the work done by the system.



```markdown
## Work Done by an Ideal Gas

For example, say you have 60 m³ of an ideal gas at 200 Pa (Pascals), and you heat it until it expands to a volume of 120 m³. How much work does the gas do? All you have to do is plug in the numbers:

$$
W = P\Delta V = (200)(120 - 60) = 12,000 \, \text{J}
$$

The gas does 12,000 Joules of work as it expands under constant pressure.

## Isochoric Process

What if the pressure in a system isn’t constant? You’re more likely to see a simple closed container, where someone has neglectfully tossed a spray can onto a fire. In this case, the volume is constant, so this is an isochoric process. As the gas inside the spray can heats up, its pressure increases, but its volume stays the same (unless, of course, the can explodes).
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

## Isochoric Process
In an isochoric process, the volume remains constant. Therefore, the work done ($W$) is zero because the area under the graph is zero.

## Isothermal Process
In an isothermal system, the temperature remains constant as other quantities change. The apparatus is designed to maintain a constant temperature of the enclosed gas, even as the piston moves. When heat is applied, the piston adjusts slowly to keep the product of pressure and volume constant.

The relationship between pressure ($P$) and volume ($V$) is given by the ideal gas law:

\[
PV = nRT
\]

From this, we can express pressure as:

\[
P = \frac{nRT}{V}
\]

The work done during an isothermal process, as the volume changes, is represented by the area under the graph. The work done is calculated using the following equation:

\[
W = nRT \ln\left(\frac{V_f}{V_o}\right)
\]

where:
- $W$ is the work done,
- $n$ is the number of moles of gas,
- $R$ is the gas constant (8.31 J/(mol·K)),
- $V_f$ is the final volume,
- $V_o$ is the initial volume,
- $\ln$ is the natural logarithm.



```markdown
## Isothermal Systems

An isothermal system maintains a constant temperature amidst other changes.

### Isothermal Graph

An isothermal graph illustrates the relationship between pressure and volume in an isothermal process.
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

Because the temperature stays constant in an isothermal process, and because the internal energy for an ideal gas equals \( \frac{3}{2} nRT \), the internal energy doesn’t change. So you have

\[
\Delta U = 0 = Q - W
\]

In other words,

\[
Q = W
\]

For example, if you have a mole of helium at a temperature of 20°C and you decide to expand it from \( V_0 = 0.010 \, \text{m}^3 \) to \( V_f = 0.020 \, \text{m}^3 \), the work done by the gas in the expansion can be calculated as follows:

\[
W = nRT \ln\left(\frac{V_f}{V_0}\right) = (1.0)(8.31)(273.15 + 20) \ln\left(\frac{0.020}{0.010}\right) = 1690 \, \text{J}
\]

The work done by the gas is 1,690 Joules. The change in the internal energy of the gas is 0 Joules, as always in an isothermal process. And because \( Q = W \), the heat added to the gas is also equal to 1,690 J.

## At Constant Heat: Adiabatic

In an adiabatic process, the total heat in a system is held constant. When a change takes place, it’s an adiabatic change.

Examining the work done during an adiabatic process, you can say \( Q = 0 \), so

\[
\Delta U = -W
\]

Because the internal energy of an ideal gas is \( U = \frac{3}{2} nRT \), the work done is

\[
W = \frac{3}{2} nR(T_0 - T_f)
\]

where \( T_0 \) represents the initial temperature and \( T_f \) represents the final temperature. If the gas does work, it comes from a change in temperature — if the temperature goes down, the gas does work on its surroundings. The work done when the total heat in the system is constant is the area under the adiabat.



```markdown
An adiabatic system doesn’t allow heat to escape or increase.

An adiabatic graph of pressure versus volume.
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

## Figuring out specific heat capacities

You can relate the initial pressure and volume to the final pressure and volume this way:

$$ P_0 V_0^\gamma = P_f V_f^\gamma $$

What’s $\gamma$? It’s the ratio of the specific heat capacity of an ideal gas at constant pressure divided by the specific heat capacity of an ideal gas at constant volume, $c_p / c_v$. The measure of how much heat an object can hold is called its specific heat capacity.

To figure out specific heat capacities, you need to relate heat, $Q$, and temperature, $T$:

$$ Q = cm\Delta T $$

where $c$ represents specific heat, $m$ represents the mass, and $\Delta T$ represents the change in temperature. In this case, however, it’s easier to talk in terms of molar specific heat capacity, which is given by $C$ and whose units are Joules/(mole-K). You use a number of moles, $n$, for molar specific heat capacity rather than the mass, $m$:

$$ Q = C n \Delta T $$

How can you solve for $C$? You must account for two different quantities, $C_p$ (constant pressure) and $C_v$ (constant volume). The first law of thermodynamics states that

$$ Q = \Delta U + W $$

so if you can get $\Delta U$ and $W$ in terms of $T$, you’re set. The work done is $P \Delta V$, so at constant volume, $W = 0$. The change in internal energy of an ideal gas is

$$ \Delta U = \frac{3}{2} n R \Delta T $$

so $Q$ at constant volume is

$$ Q_v = \frac{3}{2} n R (T_f - T_0) $$

At constant pressure, $W$ isn’t 0, because it’s $P \Delta V$. And because $PV = nRT$, you can say

$$ W = P (V_f - V_0) = n R (T_f - T_0) $$

so $Q$ at constant pressure is

$$ Q_p = \frac{3}{2} n R (T_f - T_0) + n R (T_f - T_0) $$

So, how do you get the molar specific heat capacities from this? You’ve decided that

$$ Q = C n \Delta T $$

so

$$ C = \frac{Q}{n \Delta T} $$

Dividing the above two equations by $n \Delta T$ gives you

$$ C_v = \frac{3}{2} R $$

$$ C_p = \frac{3}{2} R + R = \frac{5}{2} R $$

Now you have the molar specific heat capacities of an ideal gas. The ratio you want, $\gamma$, is the ratio of these two equations:

$$ \gamma = \frac{C_p}{C_v} = \frac{5}{3} $$



```markdown
You can connect pressure and volume at any two points along an adiabat this way:

$$ P_0 V_0^{5/3} = P_f V_f^{5/3} $$

For example, if you start with 1 liter of gas at 1 atmosphere and end up after an adiabatic change (where no heat is gained or lost) with 2 liters of gas, what would the new pressure, $P_f$, be? Putting $P_f$ on one side of the equation gives you

$$ P_f = \frac{P_0 V_0^{5/3}}{V_f^{5/3}} $$

Plugging in the numbers gives you

$$ P_f = \frac{P_0 V_0^{5/3}}{V_f^{5/3}} = \frac{(1.0)(1.0)^{5/3}}{(2.0)^{5/3}} = 0.314 \text{ atmospheres} $$

The new pressure would be 0.314 atmospheres.

### When Heat Flows: The Second Law of Thermodynamics

The second law of thermodynamics says, formally speaking, that heat flows naturally from an object at a higher temperature to an object at a lower temperature, and it doesn’t flow of its own accord in the opposite direction. The law is certainly born out of everyday observation — when was the last time you noticed an object getting colder than its surroundings by itself unless another object was doing some kind of work on it? You can force heat to flow away from an object when it would naturally flow into it if you do some work — as with refrigerators or air conditioners — but it won’t happen by itself.

### Putting heat to work: Heat engines

You have many ways to turn heat into work. You may have a steam engine, for example, that has a boiler and a set of pistons, or you may have an atomic reactor that generates superheated steam that can turn a turbine. Engines that rely on a heat source to do work are called heat engines. A heat source provides heat to the engine, which does work. And the waste heat left over is sent to a heat sink. The heat sink could be the surrounding air, or it could be a water-filled radiator, for example. As long as the heat sink is at a lower temperature than the heat source, the heat engine can do work — at least theoretically.
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

## Evaluating heat’s work: Heat engine efficiency

Heat supplied by a heat source is given the symbol $Q_h$ (for the hot source), and heat sent to a heat sink is given the symbol $Q_c$ (for the cold heat sink). With some calculation, you can find the efficiency of a heat engine. It’s the ratio of the work the engine does, $W$, to the input amount of heat — the fraction of the input heat that it converts to work:

$$
\text{Efficiency} = \frac{W}{Q_h}
$$

If all the input heat is converted to work, the efficiency will be 1.0. If no input heat is converted to work, the efficiency is 0.0. Often, the efficiency is given as a percentage, so you express these values as 100 percent and 0 percent. Because total energy is conserved, the heat into the engine must equal the work done plus the heat sent to the heat sink, which means that

$$
Q_h = W + Q_c
$$

This means that you can rewrite the efficiency in terms of just $Q_h$ and $Q_c$:

$$
\text{Efficiency} = \frac{W}{Q_h} = \frac{Q_h - Q_c}{Q_h} = 1 - \frac{Q_c}{Q_h}
$$



```markdown
Say, for example, that you have a heat engine that’s 78 percent efficient and can produce $2.55 \times 10^{7}$ Joules to clear off a roadway of water. How much heat does it use, and how much does it reject? Well, you know that

$$
\text{Efficiency} = \frac{W}{Q_h} = \frac{2.55 \times 10^{7} \text{ J}}{Q_h} = 0.78
$$

That means that

$$
Q_h = \frac{2.55 \times 10^{7} \text{ J}}{0.78} = 3.27 \times 10^{7} \text{ J}
$$

The amount of input heat is $3.27 \times 10^{7}$ Joules. So, how much heat gets left over and sent into the heat sink, $Q_c$? You know that

$$
Q_h = W + Q_c
$$

so

$$
Q_c = Q_h - W
$$

Plugging in the numbers gives you

$$
Q_c = 3.27 \times 10^{7} - 2.55 \times 10^{7} = 0.72 \times 10^{7} = Q_c
$$

The amount of heat sent to the heat sink is $0.72 \times 10^{7}$ Joules.

Carnot says you can’t have it all

Given the amount of work a heat engine does and its efficiency, you can calculate how much heat goes in and how much comes out (along with a little help from the law of conservation of energy, which ties work, heat in, and heat out together). But what about creating 100-percent-efficient heat engines? It would be nice for efficiency if you could convert all the heat that goes into a heat engine into work, but it doesn’t work out that way. Carnot says so. Heat engines have some inevitable losses in the real world, such as through friction on the pistons in a steam engine. In the 19th century, an engineer named Sadi Carnot studied this problem, and he came to the conclusion that the best you can do, effectively, is to use an engine that has no such losses.

If the engine experiences no losses, the system will return to the same state it was in before the process took place. This is called a reversible process.
```



# Chapter 15: When Heat and Work Collide: The Laws of Thermodynamics

## Carnot's Principle

Carnot's principle states that no engine that isn’t reversible can be as efficient as a reversible engine, and all reversible engines that work between the same two temperatures have the same efficiency.

## Building Carnot’s Engine

Carnot's engine operates reversibly, creating an ideal engine model. The heat from the heat source is supplied at a constant temperature, $T_h$, and the rejected heat goes into the heat sink at a constant temperature, $T_c$. The relationship between the heat provided and rejected is given by:

\[
\frac{Q_c}{Q_h} = \frac{T_c}{T_h}
\]

The efficiency of a heat engine is defined as:

\[
\text{Efficiency} = 1 - \left(\frac{Q_c}{Q_h}\right)
\]

For a Carnot engine, this becomes:

\[
\text{Efficiency} = 1 - \left(\frac{Q_c}{Q_h}\right) = 1 - \left(\frac{T_c}{T_h}\right)
\]

This equation represents the maximum possible efficiency of a heat engine. According to the third law of thermodynamics, absolute zero cannot be reached, meaning $T_c$ is never 0, and thus a 100-percent-efficient heat engine is unattainable.

## Using the Equation for a Carnot Engine

To apply the equation for maximum possible efficiency, consider a Carnot engine that connects the ground (27°C) as a heat source to the air at 33,000 feet (approximately –25°C) as the heat sink. First, convert the temperatures to Kelvin:

- Ground temperature: $T_h = 27 + 273.15 = 300.15 \, K$
- Air temperature: $T_c = -25 + 273.15 = 248.15 \, K$

Now, substituting these values into the efficiency equation:

\[
\text{Efficiency} = 1 - \left(\frac{T_c}{T_h}\right) = 1 - \left(\frac{248.15}{300.15}\right) \approx 0.173
\]

Thus, the maximum efficiency of this Carnot engine is approximately 17.3%.



```markdown
## Efficiency of a Carnot Engine

Your Carnot engine can be only 17.3 percent efficient. However, if you use the surface of the sun (about 5,800 K) as the heat source and interstellar space (about 3.4 K) as the heat sink, the efficiency can be calculated as follows:

\[
\text{Efficiency} = 1 - \left(\frac{Q_c}{Q_h}\right) = 1 - \left(\frac{T_c}{T_h}\right) = 1 - \left(\frac{3.4}{5800}\right) = 0.999
\]

This gives a theoretical efficiency for your Carnot engine of 99.9 percent.

## The Third Law of Thermodynamics

The third law of thermodynamics states that you cannot reach absolute zero through any process that uses a finite number of steps. This means that while you can lower an object's temperature closer to absolute zero, you cannot reach it completely unless you use an infinite number of steps, which is not feasible.

### Near-Zero Phenomena

Although reaching absolute zero is impossible, approaching it reveals strange phenomena. For instance, liquid helium exhibits unusual behavior at very low temperatures, such as climbing out of containers if initiated. This behavior has led to significant discoveries, earning researchers Nobel Prizes for their findings in the near-zero world.
```

```markdown
# Chapter 16: Zapping Away with Static Electricity

Electricity is built into everything around you. Each atom has its own charges moving around at incredible speeds. Sometimes, those charges become apparent when you least expect it, like when you get zapped on a dry day by touching a metal doorknob or by closing your car door. Other times, you expect those charges to be around, like when you turn on a light and you know electricity is flowing.

The atmosphere starts getting electric in this chapter, which is about the zap kind of charges — reactions that build up because you have an excess of charge (for example, when you have extra electrons sitting around). That’s static electricity. This chapter (and the next) is all about when those charges start moving, which is what you normally think of as electricity. Where you’ve got electrons, you’ve got zap, and this chapter features plenty of electrons. I discuss electric charges, electric potential, electric fields, the forces between charges, and plenty more. It all starts with electrons.

## Plus and Minus: Electron and Proton Charges

Atoms primarily consist of a nucleus containing charged protons, neutral neutrons, and lightweight electrons that orbit the nucleus at high speeds.
```



```markdown
The charged particles — electrons and protons — have the same magnitude of charge, which is

$$
e = 1.6 \times 10^{-19} \, \text{C}
$$

where C is the Coulomb, the MKS unit of charge. The charge of a proton is $+1.6 \times 10^{-19} \, \text{C}$ and the charge of an electron is $-1.6 \times 10^{-19} \, \text{C}$ (the electron’s negative charge is just convention).

So, if you have 1 entire Coulomb of charge, how many electrons do you have? Because each electron has a charge of magnitude of $1.6 \times 10^{-19} \, \text{C}$, you have

$$
\text{Total number of electrons} = \frac{1}{1.6 \times 10^{-19}} = 6.25 \times 10^{18} \, \text{electrons}
$$

You need $6.25 \times 10^{18}$ electrons to make up 1 Coulomb. But interesting things happen when you get huge numbers of electrons together. Like relatives scattering at the end of a tiresome family reunion, they fly apart.

### Push and Pull: Electric Forces

Electric charges exert a force on each other, and if you had $6.25 \times 10^{18}$ electrons, for example, you’d have a tough time keeping them together. All matter has electric charges, but if an object has an excess number of electrons, it will have a net negative charge. And if an object has a deficit of electrons, it will have a net positive charge.

As you may know from playing around with magnets, like charges repel, and unlike charges attract. The like-charged pairs of balls (++ or – –) will repel each other, but the unlike pairs of charges (+– or –+) will attract each other.
```



# Chapter 16: Zapping Away with Static Electricity

## Charging it to Coulomb’s law

Charge isn’t all positive or negative talk; you have to get numbers involved. How strong are the forces between charged objects? It all depends on how much charge is involved and on how far apart the charges are.

The electric force can be expressed as:

$$
F = \frac{k q_1 q_2}{r^2}
$$

where:
- \( F \) is the force,
- \( k \) is a constant whose value is \( 8.99 \times 10^9 \, \text{N-m}^2/\text{C}^2 \),
- \( q_1 \) and \( q_2 \) are the two charges acting on each other, measured in Coulombs,
- \( r \) is the distance between them.

Coulomb’s law gives you the magnitude of the force between charges. Note that when charges have the same sign, the force between them is positive, which means the charges repel each other; when charges have opposite signs, the force between them is negative, which means the force attracts the two charges.

Coulomb’s law can also be written in terms of a constant \( \varepsilon_0 \), called the permittivity of free space:

$$
F = \frac{q_1 q_2}{4 \pi \varepsilon_0 r^2}
$$

The constant \( \varepsilon_0 \) has a value of \( 8.854 \times 10^{-12} \, \text{C}^2/(\text{N-m}^2) \).

## Bringing objects together

The distance between objects is an important part of charge and Coulomb’s law. For example, if you separate two balls 1 meter apart and manage to give each ball 1 Coulomb of charge — one negative, one positive — you can calculate the force pulling them together using Coulomb’s law:

$$
F = \frac{(8.99 \times 10^9) (1)(1)}{(1)^2} = 8.99 \times 10^9 \, \text{N}
$$



You need $8.99 \times 10^9$ Newtons of force to hold the balls apart. That’s an unreasonably large number of Newtons — it converts to about 1.1 billion pounds or 560,000 tons, roughly the weight of 10 full oil tankers. You better rethink putting a whole Coulomb of charge on each ball. As you can see, electric forces can get pretty big when you toss whole Coulombs of charge around.

### Calculating the speed of electrons

You can relate electrostatic force and centripetal force by looking at the circular orbit of an electron. Imagine that you’re sitting around during a physics lecture messing around with a bunch of hydrogen atoms. You know that each atom is made up of a single electron circling a single proton. It’s too small to see, but you know the electron is zipping around the proton pretty fast. This realization gets you to thinking — how fast is the electron moving?

You know that the electrostatic force is between the electron and the proton, and assuming that the electron’s orbit is a circle, the electrostatic force equals the centripetal force, so you have Coulomb’s law equaling the equation for centripetal force:

\[
F = \frac{kq_1q_2}{r^2} = \frac{mv^2}{r}
\]

The mass of an electron is $9.11 \times 10^{-31}$ kg, and the radius of its orbit is $5.29 \times 10^{-11}$ m. So, plugging in numbers for the electrostatic force — the constant $k$ and the charges of the electron and proton — gives you

\[
F = \frac{(8.99 \times 10^9)(1.6 \times 10^{-19})(-1.6 \times 10^{-19})}{(5.29 \times 10^{-11})^2} = 8.22 \times 10^{-8} \, \text{N}
\]

You’ve found the force between the electron and the proton, and that force has to equal the centripetal force, so

\[
F = \frac{mv^2}{r} = \frac{(9.11 \times 10^{-31})v^2}{(5.29 \times 10^{-11})} = 8.22 \times 10^{-8} \, \text{N}
\]

Solving for $v$ gives you $2.18 \times 10^6$ meters per second, or about 4.86 million miles per hour. Try to picture that speed sometime; it’s approaching 1 percent of the speed of light.

### Looking at forces between multiple charges

Not every charge problem you come across features two charges acting on each other. Whenever you have more than two charges affecting each other, you need to use vectors to find the net force on any one charge.



```markdown
## Chapter 16: Zapping Away with Static Electricity

Take a look at Figure 16-2, which features three charges, all acting on each other — two negative charges and one positive. What’s the net force on the positive charge?

The forces on the positive charge, $Q$, due to the two negative charges, $Q_1$ and $Q_2$, are shown in Figure 16-2 as $F_1$ and $F_2$. The two forces sum up to give you $F_{\text{net}}$. Assuming $Q_1 = Q_2 = -1.0 \times 10^{-8} \, \text{C}$, $Q = 3.0 \times 10^{-8} \, \text{C}$, and the charges are at 1.0 cm on the X- and Y-axes, as shown in the figure, what’s $F_{\text{net}}$? Using the Pythagorean theorem, you know that $\theta = 45^\circ$. In magnitude, $F_1 = F_2$, so

\[
F_{\text{net}} = 2F_1 \cos 45^\circ
\]

What’s the magnitude of $F_1$?

\[
F_1 = \frac{k Q_1 Q}{r^2} = \frac{(8.99 \times 10^9) (-1.0 \times 10^{-8}) (3.0 \times 10^{-8})}{(0.01)^2} = 8.99 \times 10^{-5} \, \text{N}
\]

$F_1$ equals $8.99 \times 10^{-5} \, \text{N}$, which means you can find the net force on the positive charge:

\[
F_{\text{net}} = 2F_1 \cos 45^\circ = 2 \times 8.99 \times 10^{-5} \times \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = 1.27 \times 10^{-4} \, \text{N}
\]

The magnitude of the net force on the positive charge, found as a vector sum, is $1.27 \times 10^{-4} \, \text{N}$.
```



## Influence at a Distance: Electric Fields

To find the force between charges, you have to know the magnitude (or size) of both charges. But if you have an array of charges, you have an array of magnitudes. What if someone else wants to add a charge to the array? You don’t know how much charge the person will add — will the person bring a charge of 1.0 Coulomb to your array? How about $1.0 \times 10^{-8} \, \text{C}$ or $1.0 \times 10^{3} \, \text{C}$? Who knows?

That’s why physicists created the concept of the electric field — to describe how your array of charges will work with some other charge. All other people have to do to work with your array of charges is to multiply the electric field at any point (after you tell them what the electric field from your array is) by the charges they have. You express an electric field in Newtons per Coulomb and use the symbol $E$. (Note that an electric field is a vector quantity, meaning it has a direction and a magnitude.) Here’s how you define an electric field:

$$
E = \frac{F}{q}
$$

where $F$ represents force and $q$ represents electric charge. In other words, the electric field is the force per Coulomb at any particular point. The direction of the electric field at any one point is the force felt by a positive charge.

Say, for example, that you’re out walking your pet 1.0-Coulomb charge. It’s a sunny day, and you’re both enjoying the weather, but suddenly and unaccountably, you find yourself in a $5.0 \, \text{N/C}$ electric field pointing in the direction from which you came.

What would happen? Your pet 1.0-C charge would suddenly experience a force opposite to your direction of travel:

$$
F = qE = (1.0)(5.0) = 5.0 \, \text{Newtons}
$$



# Chapter 16: Zapping Away with Static Electricity

If you and your pet 1.0-C charge turn around, the force will be in your new direction of travel. That’s how electric fields work — someone tells you what the electric field is, and you can explain how much force would be exerted on a particular charge in that field. The force is in the same direction as the electric field at a particular point if the charge is positive, but if the charge is negative, the force is in the opposite direction.

Because the electric field at any point is a vector (meaning it has a direction and a magnitude), you can find it through vector addition. You can see this illustrated in the following equation, where two electric fields, one horizontal and one vertical, exist in the same area. The net electric field is their sum.

## Electric Fields from Point Charges

Not all electric fields you encounter look nice and even. For example, what does the electric field from a point charge look like? A point charge is just a tiny charge. You know that if you have a charge $Q$, it will create an electric field, but what’s that electric field going to be?

The equation for an electric field is given by:

\[
E = \frac{F}{q}
\]

Say, for example, that you have a test charge (a charge you use to measure forces with), $q$, and you measure the force from the charge $Q$ at a variety of points. The force you get is:

\[
F = \frac{kqQ}{r^2}
\]

So, what’s the electric field? You divide by the magnitude of your test charge, $q$:

\[
E = \frac{F}{q} = \frac{kQ}{r^2}
\]



```markdown
The magnitude of the electric field from a point charge is given by the equation:

$$
E = \frac{kQ}{r^2}
$$

The electric field is a vector, and its direction can be determined by considering a test charge, \( q \). If \( q \) is a positive charge, the electric field is defined as the force per Coulomb on this positive charge.

The force on \( q \) from a positive charge \( Q \) is directed away from \( Q \), indicating that the electric field at any point in the vicinity of \( Q \) also points away from \( Q \). Conversely, if \( Q \) is negative, the force on \( q \) will be directed toward \( Q \), and the electric field will point inward toward \( Q \).

Electric-field lines provide a qualitative representation of the electric field. When the electric-field lines are close together at a point, the electric field is stronger at that point compared to areas where the lines are further apart. Electric-field lines originate from positive charges and terminate at negative charges.

When examining the electric field from multiple charges, the total electric field at any point is the vector sum of the electric fields due to each charge. For instance, with a positive charge and a negative charge, the electric field lines will start from the positive charge and end at the negative charge, illustrating the interaction between the two charges.
```



# Chapter 16: Zapping Away with Static Electricity

## Charging nice and steady: Electric fields in parallel plate capacitors

When dealing with vectors, an electric field between multiple point charges isn’t the easiest concept to come to grips with. To simplify this, physicists developed the parallel plate capacitor. A capacitor is an object that stores charge by holding charges separate so that they attract each other, but with no way for them to go from one plate to the other by themselves.

In a parallel plate capacitor, a charge \( +q \) is spread evenly over one plate, and a charge \( -q \) is spread evenly over another. The electric field from all the point charges on these plates cancels out all components except the ones pointing between the plates. This results in constant electric fields, all in the same direction, which are easier to work with than fields from point charges.



```markdown
## Electric Field of a Parallel Plate Capacitor

A parallel plate capacitor generates an even electric field. The electric field, \( E \), between the plates is constant (as long as the plates are close enough together) and is given by the equation:

\[
E = \frac{q}{\varepsilon_0 A}
\]

where:
- \( \varepsilon_0 \) is the permittivity of free space, \( 8.854 \times 10^{-12} \, \text{C}^2/(\text{N} \cdot \text{m}^2) \),
- \( q \) is the total charge on either of the plates (one plate has charge \( +q \) and the other has charge \( -q \)),
- \( A \) is the area of each plate.

This equation can also be expressed in terms of the charge density, \( \sigma \), on each plate, where \( \sigma = \frac{q}{A} \) (the charge per square meter):

\[
E = \frac{q}{\varepsilon_0 A} = \frac{\sigma}{\varepsilon_0}
\]

Working with a parallel plate capacitor simplifies calculations because the electric field has a constant value and direction (from the positive plate to the negative plate), allowing for straightforward determination of the electric field between the plates.

## Electric Potential

Electric fields are only part of the electric story; potential energy must also be considered. This involves understanding the relationship between electric fields and electric potential, which is crucial for analyzing electrical systems.
```



```markdown
## Chapter 16: Zapping Away with Static Electricity

Stored in an object or system, mixing force and potential energy is a natural fit. For example, when you lift a weight in a gravitational field, you end up with potential energy, the energy stored in the object because of its new position:

$$
PE = mgh_f - mgh_0
$$

where \( m \) represents mass, \( g \) represents the acceleration due to gravity, \( h_f \) represents the final height, and \( h_0 \) represents the initial height. Because a force acts on the charges in an electric field, you can also speak about potential energy in electric fields. Such potential energy is electric potential energy, and a change in electric potential energy creates a new quantity called voltage, or the driving force of electric current.

### Calculating Electric Potential Energy

Electric potential energy is the potential energy stored in electric fields. Introducing the concept of energy also introduces the concept of work. Assume that you’re moving the positive charge toward the positively charged plate. The electric field can be thought of as coming from positive charges and going to negative charges, so the charge is facing a whole sheet of positive charges. Because the charge itself is positive, the force on it will push it away from the other positive charges, directing it toward the right. The sheet of negative charge behind the positive charge will also be pulling it to the right.
```



```markdown
So, how much potential energy does a positive charge acquire as it moves between the plates — going from right to left and opposing the force that would pull it back? The work you do on the charge must be equal to its gain in potential energy, and that work equals

$$
W = Fs
$$

where \( F \) represents force and \( s \) represents distance. The force on the positive charge is \( qE \), where \( q \) is the magnitude of the charge and \( E \) is the electric field the charge is in. So, the equation becomes

$$
W = qEs
$$

This equation gives you the magnitude of the potential energy the charge acquires. When the electric field is constant in direction and strength, you can say that

$$
\text{Potential Energy} = qEs
$$
```
